Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brings me in is… I don’t really feel happiness the way I used to. Or closeness, either. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but even when things should feel good, they just don’t land.

Like, my wife will try to get us back into our old Sunday routine — the kind of thing I used to look forward to — and I’ll go along with it, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. I can see that it matters to her, and I know it used to matter to me, but I can’t seem to feel that warmth or ease with her. And at work, even when something goes well, something I should be proud of as a manager, I don’t really feel satisfied. It’s more like, “Okay, that happened,” and then it’s gone.

That’s been scaring me, honestly. I don’t feel like myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much. That’s probably the biggest thing I’ve noticed, or at least the thing that scares me the most.

It’s like I can still go through the motions of things I used to enjoy, but the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. I know I used to look forward to it. I know it mattered to me. And I can tell it still matters to her, which almost makes it worse, because I can see what I’m supposed to be feeling and I just can’t reach it.

And it’s not only with her. At work, if something goes well — like a project comes together or my team handles something the right way — I can recognize that it’s good. Intellectually, I know I should feel proud or satisfied. But emotionally it’s just kind of, “Okay, that happened,” and then nothing. No real lift from it.

So yes, spending time with loved ones, things I used to enjoy, even things that should feel like accomplishments… they just don’t give me that happiness or closeness anymore. It makes me feel like I’m not really myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no. That’s part of what makes it confusing, honestly. I still show up for things. I still go along with my wife when she tries to get us back into our old Sunday routine, because I know it matters to her and I know it used to matter to me. I’ll sit there, I’ll participate, I’ll try to be present, but it’s like the part of me that used to actually enjoy it just doesn’t come online.

So from the outside, I probably look like I’m engaging. I’m not refusing everything or staying in bed or anything like that. But inside, it feels very different. The happiness doesn’t really arrive. The closeness doesn’t either. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.

It’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.

So I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.

Or at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.

So… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.

It’s more that things feel muted, or kind of empty, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely long stretches where I feel low, or heavy, or like I’m just dragging myself through the day. But if you’re asking whether it’s been most of the day nearly every day in a clear, consistent way, I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. Some days I’m more numb than sad. Some days I can function and do what I’m supposed to do, but it still doesn’t feel normal.\n\nSo… I guess I can say something has been off a lot of the time, but I’m not sure I’d know whether to call it depressed in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still try to engage in them even if they don’t feel the same?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no. That’s part of what makes it confusing, honestly. I still show up for things. I still go along with my wife when she tries to get us back into our old Sunday routine, because I know it matters to her and I know it used to matter to me. I’ll sit there, I’ll participate, I’ll try to be present, but it’s like the part of me that used to actually enjoy it just doesn’t come online.\n\nSo from the outside, I probably look like I’m engaging. I’m not refusing everything or staying in bed or anything like that. But inside, it feels very different. The happiness doesn’t really arrive. The closeness doesn’t either. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.\n\nIt’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.\n\nSo I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or moment recently where you genuinely felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.

That’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.

And at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.

So I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.

Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.

And it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.

Like with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.

So yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.

That’s probably the biggest thing I’ve noticed, or at least the thing that scares me the most.\n\nIt’s like I can still go through the motions of things I used to enjoy, but the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. I know I used to look forward to it. I know it mattered to me. And I can tell it still matters to her, which almost makes it worse, because I can see what I’m supposed to be feeling and I just can’t reach it.\n\nAnd it’s not only with her. At work, if something goes well — like a project comes together or my team handles something the right way — I can recognize that it’s good. Intellectually, I know I should feel proud or satisfied. But emotionally it’s just kind of, “Okay, that happened,” and then nothing. No real lift from it.\n\nSo yes, spending time with loved ones, things I used to enjoy, even things that should feel like accomplishments… they just don’t give me that happiness or closeness anymore. It makes me feel like I’m not really myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for a period of time recently?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling obviously depressed all day, every day, like crying or thinking, “I’m sad” from morning to night. It’s more that things feel muted, or kind of empty, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely long stretches where I feel low, or heavy, or like I’m just dragging myself through the day. But if you’re asking whether it’s been most of the day nearly every day in a clear, consistent way, I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. Some days I’m more numb than sad. Some days I can function and do what I’m supposed to do, but it still doesn’t feel normal.\n\nSo… I guess I can say something has been off a lot of the time, but I’m not sure I’d know whether to call it depressed in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still try to engage in them even if they don’t feel the same?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no. That’s part of what makes it confusing, honestly. I still show up for things. I still go along with my wife when she tries to get us back into our old Sunday routine, because I know it matters to her and I know it used to matter to me. I’ll sit there, I’ll participate, I’ll try to be present, but it’s like the part of me that used to actually enjoy it just doesn’t come online.\n\nSo from the outside, I probably look like I’m engaging. I’m not refusing everything or staying in bed or anything like that. But inside, it feels very different. The happiness doesn’t really arrive. The closeness doesn’t either. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.\n\nIt’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.\n\nSo I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or moment recently where you genuinely felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, even those you used to enjoy the most?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.\n\nAnd at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days in a row where you felt down or emotionally flat most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.\n\nAnd it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.\n\nLike with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.\n\nSo yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, even those you used to enjoy the most?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.\n\nAnd at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days in a row where you felt down or emotionally flat most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.\n\nAnd it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.\n\nLike with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.\n\nSo yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or found yourself feeling fatigued much of the time, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / manager / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean… I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it my energy level exactly, if that makes sense. It’s hard for me to separate being tired from just feeling kind of dulled out or checked out.

Like, I still get up and do what I need to do. I go through the motions. I haven’t really been tracking it as, “Oh, I’m physically exhausted today,” or “I can’t do this because I’m too tired.” It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort than it should, but I don’t know if that’s fatigue in the way you mean it.

I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed some big drop where I’m sleeping all day or unable to move around. It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but even when things should feel good, they just don’t land.\n\nLike, my wife will try to get us back into our old Sunday routine — the kind of thing I used to look forward to — and I’ll go along with it, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. That’s probably the biggest thing I’ve noticed, or at least the thing that scares me the most.\n\nIt’s like I can still go through the motions of things I used to enjoy, but the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. laugh but feeling like I don't really belong in the room with them.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a forklift backs up, a door bangs, or a plant alarm chirps, my whole body jumps like I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it as, “Oh, I’m physically exhausted today,” or “I can’t do this because I’m too tired.” It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort than it should, but I don’t know if that’s fatigue in the way you mean it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed some big drop where I’m sleeping all day or unable to move around. It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. I know I’ve said I’ve been flat a lot, and that’s true, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row,” I get a little stuck trying to measure it that precisely.

It hasn’t been like I wake up every single morning and can clearly say, “Today I am depressed from start to finish.” It’s more like… there’s this blankness or heaviness that keeps showing up, and some days it feels like it’s there almost the whole day, and other days I only notice it when I’m supposed to be feeling something and I don’t.

So I don’t want to overstate it and say yes in a perfectly clean way if I’m not sure. I’d say I’ve felt emotionally low or flat a lot, more than seems normal for me, but whether it’s been nearly every day for two straight weeks exactly… I don’t know. I haven’t been keeping track like that.

I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in how you feel about activities you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. That’s probably the biggest thing I’ve noticed, or at least the thing that scares me the most.\n\nIt’s like I can still go through the motions of things I used to enjoy, but the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. I know I used to look forward to it. I know it mattered to me. And I can tell it still matters to her, which almost makes it worse, because I can see what I’m supposed to be feeling and I just can’t reach it.\n\nAnd it’s not only with her. At work, if something goes well — like a project comes together or my team handles something the right way — I can recognize that it’s good. Intellectually, I know I should feel proud or satisfied. But emotionally it’s just kind of, “Okay, that happened,” and then nothing. No real lift from it.\n\nSo yes, spending time with loved ones, things I used to enjoy, even things that should feel like accomplishments… they just don’t give me that happiness or closeness anymore. It makes me feel like I’m not really myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for a period of time recently?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling obviously depressed all day, every day, like crying or thinking, “I’m sad” from morning to night. It’s more that things feel muted, or kind of empty, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely long stretches where I feel low, or heavy, or like I’m just dragging myself through the day. But if you’re asking whether it’s been most of the day nearly every day in a clear, consistent way, I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. Some days I’m more numb than sad. Some days I can function and do what I’m supposed to do, but it still doesn’t feel normal.\n\nSo… I guess I can say something has been off a lot of the time, but I’m not sure I’d know whether to call it depressed in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still try to engage in them even if they don’t feel the same?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no. That’s part of what makes it confusing, honestly. I still show up for things. I still go along with my wife when she tries to get us back into our old Sunday routine, because I know it matters to her and I know it used to matter to me. I’ll sit there, I’ll participate, I’ll try to be present, but it’s like the part of me that used to actually enjoy it just doesn’t come online.\n\nSo from the outside, I probably look like I’m engaging. I’m not refusing everything or staying in bed or anything like that. But inside, it feels very different. The happiness doesn’t really arrive. The closeness doesn’t either. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.\n\nIt’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.\n\nSo I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or moment recently where you genuinely felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, even those you used to enjoy the most?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.\n\nAnd at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days in a row where you felt down or emotionally flat most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.\n\nAnd it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.\n\nLike with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.\n\nSo yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or found yourself feeling fatigued much of the time, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean… I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it my energy level exactly, if that makes sense. It’s hard for me to separate being tired from just feeling kind of dulled out or checked out.\n\nLike, I still get up and do what I need to do. I go through the motions. I haven’t really been tracking it as, “Oh, I’m physically exhausted today,” or “I can’t do this because I’m too tired.” It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort than it should, but I don’t know if that’s fatigue in the way you mean it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed some big drop where I’m sleeping all day or unable to move around. It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. I wish I could give you a clean number, but I really can’t. I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything, so when you ask “how many days in a row,” I start second-guessing myself.

There have definitely been days recently where I’ve felt low or flat for most of the day. Not just a bad hour, not just getting irritated and then it passes. More like I wake up already kind of muted, go through work, come home, and by the end of the day I realize I never really came out of it. But whether that’s been three days in a row, five, a full week… I don’t know. It blurs together.

I’d say it’s happened several days close together, maybe more than once, but I’d be guessing if I gave you an exact streak. Some days it’s very noticeable, and other days I’m functional enough that I don’t realize how flat I’ve been until later. So… yes, I’ve had those kinds of days, but I don’t know the exact number in a row.

My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. I know I used to look forward to it. I know it mattered to me. And I can tell it still matters to her, which almost makes it worse, because I can see what I’m supposed to be feeling and I just can’t reach it.\n\nAnd it’s not only with her. At work, if something goes well — like a project comes together or my team handles something the right way — I can recognize that it’s good. Intellectually, I know I should feel proud or satisfied. But emotionally it’s just kind of, “Okay, that happened,” and then nothing. No real lift from it.\n\nSo yes, spending time with loved ones, things I used to enjoy, even things that should feel like accomplishments… they just don’t give me that happiness or closeness anymore. It makes me feel like I’m not really myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for a period of time recently?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling obviously depressed all day, every day, like crying or thinking, “I’m sad” from morning to night. It’s more that things feel muted, or kind of empty, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely long stretches where I feel low, or heavy, or like I’m just dragging myself through the day. But if you’re asking whether it’s been most of the day nearly every day in a clear, consistent way, I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. Some days I’m more numb than sad. Some days I can function and do what I’m supposed to do, but it still doesn’t feel normal.\n\nSo… I guess I can say something has been off a lot of the time, but I’m not sure I’d know whether to call it depressed in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still try to engage in them even if they don’t feel the same?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no. That’s part of what makes it confusing, honestly. I still show up for things. I still go along with my wife when she tries to get us back into our old Sunday routine, because I know it matters to her and I know it used to matter to me. I’ll sit there, I’ll participate, I’ll try to be present, but it’s like the part of me that used to actually enjoy it just doesn’t come online.\n\nSo from the outside, I probably look like I’m engaging. I’m not refusing everything or staying in bed or anything like that. But inside, it feels very different. The happiness doesn’t really arrive. The closeness doesn’t either. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.\n\nIt’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.\n\nSo I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or moment recently where you genuinely felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, even those you used to enjoy the most?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.\n\nAnd at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days in a row where you felt down or emotionally flat most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.\n\nAnd it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.\n\nLike with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.\n\nSo yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or found yourself feeling fatigued much of the time, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean… I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it my energy level exactly, if that makes sense. It’s hard for me to separate being tired from just feeling kind of dulled out or checked out.\n\nLike, I still get up and do what I need to do. I go through the motions. I haven’t really been tracking it as, “Oh, I’m physically exhausted today,” or “I can’t do this because I’m too tired.” It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort than it should, but I don’t know if that’s fatigue in the way you mean it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed some big drop where I’m sleeping all day or unable to move around. It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. I know I’ve said I’ve been flat a lot, and that’s true, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row,” I get a little stuck trying to measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I wake up every single morning and can clearly say, “Today I am depressed from start to finish.” It’s more like… there’s this blankness or heaviness that keeps showing up, and some days it feels like it’s there almost the whole day, and other days I only notice it when I’m supposed to be feeling something and I don’t.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it and say yes in a perfectly clean way if I’m not sure. I’d say I’ve felt emotionally low or flat a lot, more than seems normal for me, but whether it’s been nearly every day for two straight weeks exactly… I don’t know. I haven’t been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or emotionally low for most of the day, and if so, how many days in a row has this happened recently?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I really can’t. I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything, so when you ask “how many days in a row,” I start second-guessing myself.\n\nThere have definitely been days recently where I’ve felt low or flat for most of the day. Not just a bad hour, not just getting irritated and then it passes. More like I wake up already kind of muted, go through work, come home, and by the end of the day I realize I never really came out of it. But whether that’s been three days in a row, five, a full week… I don’t know. It blurs together.\n\nI’d say it’s happened several days close together, maybe more than once, but I’d be guessing if I gave you an exact streak. Some days it’s very noticeable, and other days I’m functional enough that I don’t realize how flat I’ve been until later. So… yes, I’ve had those kinds of days, but I don’t know the exact number in a row.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. I know I’ve said I’ve been flat a lot, and that’s true, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row,” I get a little stuck trying to measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I wake up every single morning and can clearly say, “Today I am depressed from start to finish.” It’s more like… there’s this blankness or heaviness that keeps showing up, and some days it feels like it’s there almost the whole day, and other days I only notice it when I’m supposed to be feeling something and I don’t.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it and say yes in a perfectly clean way if I’m not sure. I’d say I’ve felt emotionally low or flat a lot, more than seems normal for me, but whether it’s been nearly every day for two straight weeks exactly… I don’t know. I haven’t been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or emotionally low for most of the day, and if so, how many days in a row has this happened recently?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I really can’t. I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything, so when you ask “how many days in a row,” I start second-guessing myself.\n\nThere have definitely been days recently where I’ve felt low or flat for most of the day. Not just a bad hour, not just getting irritated and then it passes. More like I wake up already kind of muted, go through work, come home, and by the end of the day I realize I never really came out of it. But whether that’s been three days in a row, five, a full week… I don’t know. It blurs together.\n\nI’d say it’s happened several days close together, maybe more than once, but I’d be guessing if I gave you an exact streak. Some days it’s very noticeable, and other days I’m functional enough that I don’t realize how flat I’ve been until later. So… yes, I’ve had those kinds of days, but I don’t know the exact number in a row.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you say for sure that there have been at least two full weeks in a row recently where you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 11\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I can say that for sure. That’s the most honest answer.

I can say there have been stretches where I’ve felt low or flat a lot, and where it seemed like one day just kind of ran into the next. But “at least two full weeks in a row” and “most of the day, nearly every day” feels too exact for me to honestly confirm. I haven’t been tracking it that way, and my memory of it gets kind of smeared together.

Some days I know it was there for most of the day. Other days I was functioning and doing what I had to do, and I might not have noticed how low I felt until later. So I don’t want to say yes just because it feels like it’s been happening a lot. I’d say I’m not sure. I can’t confidently say it was two full weeks straight.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I don’t really feel happiness the way I used to. Or closeness, either. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but even when things should feel good, they just don’t land.\n\nLike, my wife will try to get us back into our old Sunday routine — the kind of thing I used to look forward to — and I’ll go along with it, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. I can see that it matters to her, and I know it used to matter to me, but I can’t seem to feel that warmth or ease with her. And at work, even when something goes well, something I should be proud of as a manager, I don’t really feel satisfied. It’s more like, “Okay, that happened,” and then it’s gone.\n\nThat’s been scaring me, honestly. I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in how you feel about activities you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. That’s probably the biggest thing I’ve noticed, or at least the thing that scares me the most.\n\nIt’s like I can still go through the motions of things I used to enjoy, but the feeling doesn’t come with it anymore. My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. I know I used to look forward to it. I know it mattered to me. And I can tell it still matters to her, which almost makes it worse, because I can see what I’m supposed to be feeling and I just can’t reach it.\n\nAnd it’s not only with her. At work, if something goes well — like a project comes together or my team handles something the right way — I can recognize that it’s good. Intellectually, I know I should feel proud or satisfied. But emotionally it’s just kind of, “Okay, that happened,” and then nothing. No real lift from it.\n\nSo yes, spending time with loved ones, things I used to enjoy, even things that should feel like accomplishments… they just don’t give me that happiness or closeness anymore. It makes me feel like I’m not really myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for a period of time recently?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling obviously depressed all day, every day, like crying or thinking, “I’m sad” from morning to night. It’s more that things feel muted, or kind of empty, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely long stretches where I feel low, or heavy, or like I’m just dragging myself through the day. But if you’re asking whether it’s been most of the day nearly every day in a clear, consistent way, I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. Some days I’m more numb than sad. Some days I can function and do what I’m supposed to do, but it still doesn’t feel normal.\n\nSo… I guess I can say something has been off a lot of the time, but I’m not sure I’d know whether to call it depressed in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, or do you still try to engage in them even if they don’t feel the same?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped doing them, no. That’s part of what makes it confusing, honestly. I still show up for things. I still go along with my wife when she tries to get us back into our old Sunday routine, because I know it matters to her and I know it used to matter to me. I’ll sit there, I’ll participate, I’ll try to be present, but it’s like the part of me that used to actually enjoy it just doesn’t come online.\n\nSo from the outside, I probably look like I’m engaging. I’m not refusing everything or staying in bed or anything like that. But inside, it feels very different. The happiness doesn’t really arrive. The closeness doesn’t either. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.\n\nIt’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.\n\nSo I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or moment recently where you genuinely felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, even those you used to enjoy the most?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.\n\nAnd at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days in a row where you felt down or emotionally flat most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.\n\nAnd it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.\n\nLike with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.\n\nSo yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or found yourself feeling fatigued much of the time, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean… I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it my energy level exactly, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it as, “Oh, I’m physically exhausted today,” or “I can’t do this because I’m too tired.” It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort than it should, but I don’t know if that’s fatigue in the way you mean it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed some big drop where I’m sleeping all day or unable to move around. It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. I can remember that those things used to feel warm and normal and easy, especially with my wife, but now I’m sort of watching myself do them without getting the feeling from them.\n\nIt’s similar at work. If something goes well, I still do my job, I still handle things, and I can tell when something is objectively a good outcome. But I don’t get that sense of satisfaction or pride I used to get. It’s more like I register it in my head, but emotionally there’s just not much there.\n\nSo I guess the answer is: I still try. I’m still engaging in things. But they don’t feel the same at all, and that’s what’s been really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any activity or moment recently where you genuinely felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: Honestly… I’m trying to think of one, and that’s what bothers me. I can think of moments that should have been enjoyable. Like my wife trying to get us back into those old Sunday routines — the kind of thing that used to make me feel close to her, like we were just being us again. And I want to enjoy it. I really do. I’ll notice little things and think, “This is supposed to feel nice. This used to feel nice.” But the actual pleasure doesn’t really come.\n\nOr at work, if something goes well, I can see that it went well. I can even say the right thing, like, “Good job,” or “That’s great,” and I mean it in my head. But I don’t feel that real lift, that satisfaction, the pride or happiness I used to get from it. It’s more like I’m observing that a good thing happened rather than feeling good about it.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there’s been a recent moment where I genuinely felt pleasure, even briefly, I’m struggling to name one. Maybe there are tiny flashes that pass so quickly I barely catch them, but nothing that feels real or solid. Mostly it feels like I’m going through things that should make me happy or connected, and I just can’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, even those you used to enjoy the most?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or very close to that. “Complete” is a hard word for me, because I can still recognize that something is supposed to be good. I can still understand it logically. But the actual pleasure part, the part where I feel happy or close or satisfied, that’s mostly just not there anymore.\n\nThat’s really what brings me in, honestly. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routines, the things we used to enjoy together, and I’ll go along with it. I want it to feel normal. I want to feel connected to her. But I’m sitting there almost waiting for the feeling to show up, and it just… doesn’t. It’s like I remember what it used to feel like, but I can’t access it.\n\nAnd at work, it’s the same sort of thing. If something goes well, I know it went well. I can say, “That’s good,” and I mean that in a practical sense. But I don’t feel the satisfaction or pride that I used to get. There’s no real lift from it.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say every single second of every single activity, but in the things that used to matter most to me, I don’t feel real happiness or closeness anymore. That’s the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days in a row where you felt down or emotionally flat most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always like I’m crying or visibly upset or anything like that. A lot of the time it’s more like I’m emotionally flat, like I’m there but not really feeling much of anything.\n\nAnd it has been most days, honestly. That’s part of why I finally came in. It’s not just one bad afternoon or one rough weekend. It’s been this repeated thing where I go through the day, do what I’m supposed to do, talk to people, go home, try to be with my wife, and there’s just this blankness where happiness or closeness should be.\n\nLike with our old Sunday routines — my wife keeps trying, and I go along because I want to feel it again. I want to enjoy being with her the way I used to. But I’m sitting there thinking, “This should feel good,” and it just doesn’t really reach me. And at work, even when something goes well, I can recognize that it’s good, but I don’t feel the satisfaction from it.\n\nSo yes, over the past couple weeks, it’s been there most of the day on a lot of days. Maybe “down” isn’t always the exact word. Sometimes it’s more numb or flat than sad. But it’s been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or found yourself feeling fatigued much of the time, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean… I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it my energy level exactly, if that makes sense. It’s hard for me to separate being tired from just feeling kind of dulled out or checked out.\n\nLike, I still get up and do what I need to do. I go through the motions. I haven’t really been tracking it as, “Oh, I’m physically exhausted today,” or “I can’t do this because I’m too tired.” It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort than it should, but I don’t know if that’s fatigue in the way you mean it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed some big drop where I’m sleeping all day or unable to move around. It’s not that obvious. But I also don’t feel energetic, really. I guess I’d say I’m not sure. There’s a kind of heaviness to getting through the day, but I haven’t thought of it specifically as being fatigued from doing nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. I know I’ve said I’ve been flat a lot, and that’s true, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row,” I get a little stuck trying to measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I wake up every single morning and can clearly say, “Today I am depressed from start to finish.” It’s more like… there’s this blankness or heaviness that keeps showing up, and some days it feels like it’s there almost the whole day, and other days I only notice it when I’m supposed to be feeling something and I don’t.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it and say yes in a perfectly clean way if I’m not sure. I’d say I’ve felt emotionally low or flat a lot, more than seems normal for me, but whether it’s been nearly every day for two straight weeks exactly… I don’t know. I haven’t been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or emotionally low for most of the day, and if so, how many days in a row has this happened recently?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I really can’t. I haven’t been marking it on a calendar or anything, so when you ask “how many days in a row,” I start second-guessing myself.\n\nThere have definitely been days recently where I’ve felt low or flat for most of the day. Not just a bad hour, not just getting irritated and then it passes. More like I wake up already kind of muted, go through work, come home, and by the end of the day I realize I never really came out of it. But whether that’s been three days in a row, five, a full week… I don’t know. It blurs together.\n\nI’d say it’s happened several days close together, maybe more than once, but I’d be guessing if I gave you an exact streak. Some days it’s very noticeable, and other days I’m functional enough that I don’t realize how flat I’ve been until later. So… yes, I’ve had those kinds of days, but I don’t know the exact number in a row.\n\nDoctor: Can you say for sure that there have been at least two full weeks in a row recently where you felt down or emotionally low most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can say that for sure. That’s the most honest answer.\n\nI can say there have been stretches where I’ve felt low or flat a lot, and where it seemed like one day just kind of ran into the next. But “at least two full weeks in a row” and “most of the day, nearly every day” feels too exact for me to honestly confirm. I haven’t been tracking it that way, and my memory of it gets kind of smeared together.\n\nSome days I know it was there for most of the day. Other days I was functioning and doing what I had to do, and I might not have noticed how low I felt until later. So I don’t want to say yes just because it feels like it’s been happening a lot. I’d say I’m not sure. I can’t confidently say it was two full weeks straight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, that’s actually one of the main things that made me think something is really wrong.

It’s not just that I’m bored or tired of certain hobbies. It’s more like the part of me that’s supposed to feel enjoyment doesn’t fully come online anymore. My wife will try to get us to do our old Sunday routine — the kind of thing I used to genuinely look forward to — and I can go through the motions. I can sit there, I can say the right things, I can recognize that this used to be nice. But I don’t really feel that warmth from it. I don’t feel close in the way I think I should.

And even at work, if something goes well, something that I would normally take pride in, it’s like I register it in my head but not anywhere deeper. I can think, “Okay, that was a good outcome,” but there’s no real satisfaction behind it. Maybe for a second I can understand that I ought to feel good, but it doesn’t last, and sometimes it doesn’t show up at all.

So yes, I’d say I’ve lost a lot of that. Happiness, satisfaction, closeness… it all feels muted or kind of out of reach.

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but even when things should feel good, they just don’t land.\n\nLike, my wife will try to get us back into our old Sunday routine — the kind of thing I used to look forward to — and I’ll go along with it, but inside it’s like there’s nothing there. My wife tries to get us back into our old Sunday routines — you know, the kind of ordinary stuff that used to feel comforting, like we had our little rhythm together — and I’ll be there physically, I’ll participate, I’ll say the right things, but inside it just feels… flat. I know I used to look forward to it. I know it mattered to me.
